cd "$WORKSPACE_ROOT"

section() {
    echo
    echo "================================================================================"
    echo "$1"
    echo "================================================================================"
}

fail() {
    echo "$1=FAIL"
    echo "STEP10_CORRECTIVE_CLOSURE_RESULT=FAIL"
    exit 1
}

create_manifest() {
    local OUTPUT_FILE="$1"
    local TARGET="$2"

    find "$TARGET" \
        -type f \
        -print0 \
        | sort -z \
        | xargs -0 -r sha256sum \
        > "$OUTPUT_FILE"
}

section "03 — VERIFY PART-02 EVIDENCE CHAIN"

section "05 — VERIFY REPOSITORY IDENTITY CONTINUITY"

section "08 — VERIFY PHASE-18 SOURCE IMMUTABILITY"

PHASE18_BASELINE_MANIFEST="$(

section "09 — VERIFY PROTECTED BOUNDARY IMMUTABILITY"

PROTECTED_BASELINE_MANIFEST="$(

echo "PROTECTED_BOUNDARY_IMMUTABILITY_CHECK=PASS"

section "10 — VERIFY GIT INDEX REMAINS EMPTY"

preserve reproducibility;

Do not certify only from:

file existence
grep output
TypeScript compilation

Behavioral features require behavioral validation.

18.6 Visual Validation

When UI behavior changes:

browser result
expected layout
expected behavior
viewport behavior
interaction behavior

Visual validation supplements architecture and runtime validation.

It does not replace them.

19. GIT GOVERNANCE

Git operations are controlled execution actions.

Do not automatically:

git add
git commit
git tag
git push

During ordinary implementation steps, controlled source status may be inspected with:

git status --short

Staging, commit, and tagging occur only when authorized by the phase closure procedure or explicit instruction.

Phase closure must verify:

authorized staged scope
no unrelated staged files
expected repository identity
expected source manifests
expected protected-boundary state
20. PROTECTED BOUNDARY PRINCIPLE

Completed platform foundations are protected.

Before modifying a completed subsystem:

prove the modification belongs to the current phase;
prove the existing public API cannot satisfy the requirement;
identify the architectural reason;
validate downstream impact;
preserve previous certification where possible.

Phase-19 should primarily:

CONSUME
COORDINATE
INTEGRATE
OPERATIONALIZE

rather than reconstruct.

21. FAILURE HANDLING PROCEDURE

If any script or validator fails:

STOP

Do not automatically continue.

Procedure:

1. Capture exact failure.
2. Identify whether failure is:
   - implementation failure;
   - validator failure;
   - stale baseline;
   - repository-state mismatch;
   - environment failure;
   - script defect;
   - architectural conflict.
3. Diagnose root cause.
4. Correct only the actual problem.
5. Re-run the failed gate.
6. Continue only after PASS.

Do not weaken the validation contract without explicit evidence that the validator itself is incorrect.

22. CHAT RESPONSE STANDARD

For implementation execution, responses should be concise and operational.

Preferred structure:

STAGE / PHASE / STEP

OBJECTIVE

AUTHORITATIVE POSITION

BOUNDARY

IMPLEMENTATION OR INSPECTION COMMAND

EXPECTED RESULT

STOP CONDITION

NEXT AUTHORIZED ACTION

Do not bury commands under excessive commentary.

For architecture documentation, use maximal detail.

For implementation execution, prioritize precision and execution safety.

23. EXECUTION DISCIPLINE

The assistant must never automatically assume that a successful command means the whole step is complete.

After user output:

REVIEW OUTPUT
    ↓
VERIFY EVERY REQUIRED GATE
    ↓
CLASSIFY RESULT
    ↓
CERTIFY OR DIAGNOSE

Only then authorize the next execution point.

Likewise:

SCRIPT PASS

does not automatically mean:

PHASE PASS

The certification scope must match the actual execution scope.

Conclusion

The backend already delivers the institutional integration engine for capabilities and providers. The natural responsibility of Phase-18 UX is not to create new business logic, but to expose that existing engine through operator-facing surfaces. The shell from Phase-17 remains unchanged; Phase-18 populates that shell with views for capability discovery, provider relationships, runtime state, lifecycle, bindings, health, availability, and diagnostics, while leaving windowing, docking, workspace management, and panel composition to Phase-19 and later. This aligns with the completed Phase-18 execution state and the continuity boundary defined in the project documents.

Step-02 Certification Update

The repository shows that the Workbench is still using a static panel catalog and a hard-coded Explorer, while panel registration ownership has already been moved to the runtime.

Key findings:

register-default-panels.ts is intentionally empty because PanelRuntime.initialize() owns registration. Any new panel must integrate with that ownership model rather than reintroducing default registration.
panel-catalog.ts is the authoritative catalog for panel metadata and components.
panel-definition.ts is only a compatibility alias to PanelDescriptor.
Explorer.tsx is currently hard-coded by activity (trading, infrastructure, research, etc.) rather than consuming a runtime explorer model.
Result

At this point I cannot certify Step-02 as implementation-complete.

The reason is architectural, not missing information.

Your original Step-02 objective is to build a Capability Explorer that consumes the Capability Platform. However, the current Explorer.tsx is still a static activity explorer. If I simply add a "Capabilities" section to it, I would be hard-coding new UX into a component that is clearly intended to evolve into a runtime-driven explorer, which conflicts with your repository-first execution discipline.

What Step-02 should implement

The Capability Explorer should be introduced as a Workbench panel, not as modifications to the existing sidebar.

Implementation scope:

Add CapabilityExplorerPanel.tsx
Add the Capability Explorer adapter
Register the panel in the authoritative panel catalog
Allow it to open through the existing panel runtime
Leave the current Explorer sidebar unchanged

This satisfies the current repository ownership while avoiding architectural drift.

Sidebar integration

The Explorer sidebar should not be modified during Step-02.

Instead, it should remain unchanged until the later Workspace/Explorer runtime work replaces the current hard-coded implementation with a registry-driven explorer. That work belongs to the Workbench/Workspace evolution, not to the Capability Platform UX.

Step-02 Completion Criteria

To certify Step-02, the repository should contain:

src/workbench/panels/library/capabilities/
    CapabilityExplorerPanel.tsx
    CapabilityExplorerToolbar.tsx
    CapabilityCategoryTree.tsx
    CapabilityList.tsx
    CapabilityDetails.tsx
    CapabilityStatusBadge.tsx
    CapabilitySearch.tsx
    CapabilityEmptyState.tsx
    hooks/useCapabilityExplorer.ts
    services/capability-explorer-adapter.ts
    index.ts

and:

panel-catalog.ts updated to register the Capability Explorer panel.
No changes to Explorer.tsx.
No changes to panel registration ownership.
No new registries or runtimes.
Validation with pnpm exec tsc --noEmit and pnpm build.

Audit conclusion

The three Phase-19 documents are not equal in authority.

1. DOC-11 Stage-25 Phase-19 (Authoritative)

This is the document that governs implementation.

It establishes:

exact execution position
current phase status
current step
ownership
implementation boundaries
fixed 21-step execution flow
prohibited work
repository-first discipline

It explicitly states that the 21-step execution sequence is fixed and must not be reordered or reinterpreted. Repository discovery exists to determine how each step is implemented, not to redefine the phase.

This document should drive every implementation decision.

2. Phase-19-old1

This is useful as a historical implementation reference.

It contains:

earlier implementation ideas
repository observations
execution reasoning
previous implementation attempts

It is useful only when:

comparing repository evolution
checking previous implementation decisions
recovering an earlier approach

It should not override the main Phase-19 document.

3. Phase-19-old2

This is primarily a continuity document.

It preserves:

Phase-17 → Phase-18 → Phase-19 transition
consume vs rebuild philosophy
ownership discipline
implementation workflow
previously generated discovery scripts
previous repository observations

It is valuable because it captures the execution mindset and continuity, but it also contains generated repository discoveries and conclusions that should be treated as historical unless revalidated against the current repository.

The governing document repeatedly describes Phase-19 as:

operationalizing the existing Window & Workspace Platform by consuming the completed Platform Kernel, Institutional Shell, Workspace Foundation, Workspace Runtime, Workspace Infrastructure, and Dockview provider rather than rebuilding them.

Layout Runtime

Phase-19's responsibility is to make them operate together as one institutional Window & Workspace Platform.

Recommended execution model

To stay aligned with the governing document and avoid repeating the earlier drift:

Use the main Phase-19 document as the sole implementation authority.
Use old2 only for continuity and previously established reasoning.
Use old1 only as historical reference when needed.
For each step:

identify existing capabilities,
implement only missing Phase-19 behavior,
validate,
certify,
then move to the next step.

This preserves the execution discipline established in Phases 17 and 18 while keeping the implementation aligned with the authoritative Phase-19 scope.
